<meta>This title page introduces a religious work published in 1699. Significant ink bleed-through from the back of the page is visible, which was common in 17th-century printing. The book belongs to the "emblematic" tradition, where visual images were used to illustrate spiritual concepts.</meta>

->The<-

# Spiritual Imaginations of the Heart,

->In two hundred and fifty<-

# Biblical Pictorial Sayings
<note>The original German "Figur-Sprüchen" refers to a "rebus"—a devotional puzzle where specific words in a biblical verse are replaced by small illustrations. This was a popular method for teaching scripture and encouraging meditation.</note>

->presented:<-

## First Part.

---

->Available from<-
->**Johann Simon Negges, in Augsburg.**<-
<note>Augsburg was a major center for printing and copperplate engraving in the Holy Roman Empire during this period.</note>

-><insert>1699.</insert><-

<summary>This is the title page of a 1699 devotional book titled "Spiritual Imaginations of the Heart," featuring 250 biblical rebuses published by Johann Simon Negges in Augsburg.</summary>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues from the title page of the 1699 work "Spiritual Imaginations of the Heart." The text presented here is an introductory poem, likely a preface, explaining the spiritual and educational purpose of the "Bible Saying Figures" (rebuses) contained in the book. It emphasizes the use of visual aids to engage the "outer senses" and lead the reader—especially the youth—toward internal piety.</meta>

If heart and eye observe these biblical pictorial sayings,
They find within them the most pleasant traces
Of God's holiness and His soul's desire, <note>original: "Seel Begierd"; this refers to God's deep longing for the human soul.</note>
And how His precious word leads as a path to heaven.
Whoever lets this remain their guideline at all times
And writes it upon their soft heart as on a tablet,
Shall be refreshed and delighted in their soul,
And through the external senses, the spirit is set in joy.
Here, here the taste can have the heavenly manna,
Which will most lovely refresh the soul;
The eye beholds here the true sunlight,
Whose clarity no darkness interrupts.
When through the ear into the heart the words of life fall,
It resembles the sweetest tone and the ringing of music.
The fragrance of flowers provides pleasure to the nose,
Yet more does the word's life-force refresh the heart.
Should sensitivity count on gentle rest,
Here is what only pleases, to be felt in richest measure.
Bread is praised as strength, yet only for the body alone;
The word, the food of the soul, shall be eternally healing.
The outstretched hand serves those who travel;
To Christians, the word wants to show the road to heaven;
The fresh spring refreshes the weary body;
For the soul, the stream of comfort flows here ever bright.
If the body falls ill, it finds rescue through medicines;
The word in spiritual distress <note>original: "Seelen-Noth"; a state of existential or religious crisis.</note> brings refreshment and prosperity.

<column-break/>

In physical danger, shield and sword protect;
The power of the enemy of the soul <note>A reference to the Devil or temptation.</note> is destroyed through the word.
Who should not seek or love such a treasure,
And practice reading it both day and night?
Who should so neglect their soul's salvation,
Despising that which makes them happy for eternity?
Since now, moreover, this noble heavenly food
Is set before us here in a new way,
And alongside the mind, delights the outer senses,
All weariness and disgust vanish entirely.
Intermingled with pictures, many a saying appears here,
Since this work is intended for the benefit of the youth,
That through the eye into the heart the core might penetrate in good time
And through the grace of the Most High, bring many blessed fruits.
A beautiful pastime, well-suited for children,
This Bible-work may rightly be called,
Since with amusement, the word's dear treasure
Most edifyingly finds a place in the still tender breast.
The healing success, the wonderful trials,
These will praise this work with much utility;
Through its service, it will become known more and more
(At which it solely aims) the praise and honor of the Most High.
Happy is he who delights in looking at these images
And seeks to edify his children through them as well;
The fruit of this looking shall still be known there [in heaven],
Where the joy of beholding God <note>original: "Anschau-Lust"; a theological term for the "Beatific Vision," or the ultimate joy of seeing God in the afterlife.</note> delights us eternally.

<summary>This introductory poem justifies the use of "pictorial sayings" as a tool for religious education, arguing that engaging the physical senses (sight, taste, hearing) helps internalize divine truths, particularly for children. It positions the book as both a pleasant pastime and a vital spiritual medicine against the "enemy of the soul."</summary>
<keywords>piety, religious education, rebuses, spiritual senses, emblems, 17th-century poetry, Beatific Vision, didactic literature</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the introductory poem to the formal prose preface of the work. It establishes the geographical and artistic context of the book’s production in Augsburg and its intended purpose as a tool for both aesthetic pleasure and moral instruction.</meta>

# A Short Preface Concerning the Biblical Pictorial Sayings.

-> To the Greatly Favorable and Highly Gracious / <-
-> Art-Lovers and Viewers of these Biblical Pictorial Sayings, <-
-> who are to be highly honored according to their social standing. <-

That Augsburg, above many other cities, has reached a very high degree of excellence through the ingenious diligence of excellent virtuosos in the noble **art of copperplate engraving** <term>Kupfferstecher-Kunst: the process of incising a design onto a copper plate, which is then inked and pressed to create detailed prints.</term> and currently remains in a state of daily increasing prosperity, requires all the less a lengthy proof, as the clearer and more numerous incomparable examples of it are plain to behold and are distributed through all parts of the world. While each of these works is granted its own value and praise according to its merits, all impartial observers will agree that the greatest glory belongs to those who, alongside the pleasure of the eyes, also aim for the **edification of the soul** <term>Gemüths-Erbauung: the spiritual, moral, or emotional improvement of the reader’s inner self.</term> and practical benefit through their artistic practice; and among this latter group, the present work deserves no lowly place. 

How graciously and joyfully this beautiful work has been received by Christian souls—and especially by parents who care for the soul-nourishing education of their tender children—has been shown by its frequent sale; since, at this time, almost all copies have been distributed <note>original: "Exemplaria distrahiret"; a Latin-derived phrase indicating that the print run has been sold out or dispersed among buyers.</note>, the publisher has wished to undertake the costs anew, especially for the benefit of the dear youth, and to publish this most useful work; so that it

<summary>This preface introduces the book as a product of Augsburg's famous engraving industry, asserting that while the art is beautiful, its true value lies in its power to provide spiritual education for children. The text notes that the book was so popular it sold out, necessitating this new edition.</summary>
<keywords>Augsburg, copperplate engraving, spiritual edification, religious education, children, publishing, 17th-century art</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1699 edition of "Spiritual Imaginations of the Heart." It serves as a poetic preface, explaining the pedagogical and spiritual purpose of the "rebus" format (mixing text and images) to the reader. The text emphasizes engaging all five senses to internalize scripture.</meta>

If heart and eye behold these biblical pictorial sayings,
They find within them the most pleasant traces
Of **God's** holiness and our desire for salvation,
And how His precious Word leads as a path to heaven.
Whoever lets this remain their guiding rule at all times
And writes it upon their soft heart as if upon a tablet,
His soul shall be refreshed and delighted,
And through the outer senses, the spirit shall be set in joy.
Here, here the taste can have the Heavenly Manna,
Which shall refresh the soul in the loveliest way;
The eye perceives here the true light of the sun,
Whose clarity no darkness can ever interrupt.
When the words of life fall through the ear into the heart,
It is like the sweetest tone and the sounding of music.
The fragrance of flowers brings pleasure to the nose,
Yet the life-force of the Word refreshes the heart even more.
If the sense of touch should count on gentle rest,
Here is that which satisfies, felt in the richest measure.
<note>The author is using the five senses—sight, taste, hearing, smell, and touch—as a metaphor for how a believer should "consume" and experience the Bible.</note>
Bread is praised as strength, yet only for the body alone;
The Word, the food of the soul, shall be eternally healing.
The outstretched hand serves those who are traveling;
The Word wishes to show the Christian the road to heaven.
The fresh spring refreshes the exhausted body;
For the soul, the brook of comfort flows ever brightly here.
If the body falls ill, it finds rescue through medicines;
The Word brings relief and prosperity in the soul’s distress.

In physical danger, shield and sword protect;
The power of the soul’s enemy <note>original: "Seelen-Feind"; a reference to the Devil or spiritual temptation.</note> is destroyed through the Word.
Who would not seek or love such a treasure,
And practice reading it both day and night?
Who would leave the salvation of their soul so out of mind,
Despising that which makes them happy for eternity?
Since now, moreover, the noble Heavenly Food
Is presented here in a new way,
And delights the outer senses alongside the mind,
All weariness and loathing completely vanish.
Intermingled with pictures, many a saying appears here,
As this work is intended for the benefit of the youth,
So that through the eye, the kernel may penetrate the heart in good time
And, through the grace of the Highest, bring much blessed fruit.
A beautiful pastime, well-suited for children,
This Bible-work may be called with every right,
Where, with delight, the precious treasure of the Word
Finds an edifying place within the still-tender breast.
The healing success, the wonderful examples,
These shall praise this work with much utility;
Through its service, more and more shall be made known
(Which is its only aim) the praise and honor of the Highest.
Blessed is he who delights in the viewing of these pictures
And seeks to build up his children through them as well;
The fruit of this "beholding" shall still be known there,
Where the joyful contemplation of **God** <term>original: "GOttes-Anschau-Lust"; referring to the "Beatific Vision," the ultimate direct self-communication of God to the individual.</term> delights us eternally.

<summary>This poetic preface explains that the book's use of rebuses (mixing images and text) is designed to engage the senses and make scripture more accessible and memorable, particularly for children and young people. It frames the act of reading and "beholding" these puzzles as a spiritual exercise that prepares the soul for the eternal vision of God.</summary>
<keywords>preface, religious education, rebuses, Five Senses, devotional poetry, Beatific Vision, children's education, 17th-century spirituality</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the introductory poem to the formal prose preface of the work. The previous page concluded a poetic meditation on how the senses (sight, hearing, smell) should be directed toward God's word. This page begins the "Short Preliminary Report," establishing the book’s origin in Augsburg—a center of the engraving arts—and its purpose as an educational tool for children.</meta>

# A Short Preliminary Report regarding the Biblical Pictorial-Verses.

## Very Generous and Highly Favored /

### Most Honorable Art-Lovers according to their rank, and
### Viewers of these Biblical Pictorial-Verses.

That Augsburg, above many other cities, through the ingenious diligence of excellent virtuosos <note>original: "Virtuosen"; refers to highly skilled master artists.</note> in the noble art of copper engraving, <term>copper engraving</term> <note>A printing technique where images are incised into a metal plate; Augsburg was the leading European center for this craft in the 17th century.</note> has brought this art to a very high level and still currently stands in a daily increasing state of prosperity, requires a lengthy proof all the less, as the clearer and more numerous incomparable examples of this lie before our eyes and are carried through all parts of the world. One leaves each of these to its own worth and praise according to its merits; yet all impartial observers will agree that the greatest glory is due to those who, alongside the delight of the eyes, also aimed for the edification of the soul <note>original: "Gemüths-Erbauung"; the process of strengthening one's spiritual and moral character.</note> and practical benefit through the exercise of their art; and among this latter group, the present work does not deserve the lowest place. 

How favorably and joyfully this beautiful work has been received by Christian souls—and especially by parents who care for the soul-healing edification of their tender children—has been shown by the frequent sale <note>original: "Abgang"; referring to the commercial success and depletion of the stock.</note> of the same. Because nearly all copies <note>original: "Exemplaria"; Latin plural for copies of a book.</note> have been sold out <note>original: "distrahiret"; from the Latin 'distrahere,' meaning to sell or distribute.</note> at this time, the publisher has wished to invest the costs anew, especially for the benefit of the dear youth, and to issue this most useful work; so that

<summary>This preface introduces the book as a product of Augsburg's famous copper engraving industry, emphasizing that the work is designed for both artistic beauty and the spiritual education of children.</summary>
<keywords>Augsburg, copper engraving, religious education, rebuses, children's books, spiritual edification, 17th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal preface, explaining the mechanical structure of the book—including its 250 biblical verses and pictorial rebuses—and its pedagogical value for children's education and language development.</meta>

That it deserves this title, however, the following brief explanation will make credible. Specifically, as previously mentioned, there are 250 **Biblical Verses** <term>Biblische Sprüche: short passages or maxims from the Bible, often used for moral instruction.</term> contained in this first part, interspersed with elegantly engraved figures, where the verse in the center is always taken according to the order of the books of **Holy Scripture** <term>S. Schrifft: abbreviation for "Heilige Schrift," meaning the Bible.</term>.

Then, for the service of those who might not have a Bible at hand, and also to prevent false interpretation or incorrect pronunciation, all occurring verses are printed separately and word-for-word beforehand; therein, the figure is always expressed with **red letters**, which the inexperienced can use as a **key**. The frequently occurring **hearts** <term>Hertzen: a common symbol in 18th-century devotional art, representing the human soul or the seat of faith.</term> are enclosed in this first part with **wreaths** <term>Kräntzen: decorative circular arrangements.</term> of all kinds of plants, flowers, and herbs, according to their nature, and a special index regarding the **coloration** <term>Coloriten: the arrangement or choice of colors in a work of art.</term> is placed before them. Art-loving young people may well benefit from the neat handwriting, good drawings, and clean strokes, etc. 

The main objective, aiming for **God’s honor and the pleasant instruction of youth**, will recommend this work without further boasting, when the outcome shows, to the heartfelt delight of Christian parents, how through this God-pleasing pastime even small children learn the most beautiful verses by heart as if **playing**, how idle hours are usefully shortened, how they so gracefully exercise and demonstrate their **judgment** <term>Judicium: the faculty of discernment or critical thinking.</term> and at the same time, through the help of the beautiful figures, obtain an **idea** <term>Ideam oder Begriff: a conceptual image or mental representation.</term> or concept of many things that would not have come to their sight for a long time; the early recognition of these things will be highly beneficial to them for learning the language and for the precise understanding of what they hear and read. In the undoubted hope of desired success and favorable approval and reception of this work (which has also been **illuminated** <term>illuminirt: hand-colored, usually with watercolors, to enhance the detail of the engravings.</term> for even greater delight and a deeper impression on the power of the imagination), the author recommends this and his other publications to art-lovers with becoming respect.

->The Publisher.<-

<summary>The publisher explains the book's structure, featuring 250 illustrated biblical verses designed as a "key" to help children learn scripture through play and visual association. It emphasizes the pedagogical benefits of using engravings to teach vocabulary, concepts, and moral judgment.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, copperplate engraving, religious education, rebuses, emblem book, children's literature, Augsburg, biblical verses, illustration, hand-coloring</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page provides the organizational logic for the "picture-sayings" mentioned on the previous page. It explains how the three verses on each page are grouped (one primary verse in biblical order, flanked by two supporting verses) and provides an index for the reader to locate specific books of the Bible within the collection.</meta>

# First Index of the Biblical Picture-Sayings.

For further explanation of these Biblical Sayings, it is first necessary to inform well-disposed enthusiasts regarding their arrangement. This work is organized according to the books of **Holy Scripture** <term>H. Schrifft: abbreviation for "Heilige Schrift," the German term for the Bible.</term>, such that on every page, the middle saying of the three is the one that follows the biblical order. The other two flanking it are from different books, selected and added according to how well they complement the middle one. While the total number of books in Holy Scripture (excluding a few **Apocrypha** <term>Apocrypha: religious texts included in some versions of the Bible, like the Catholic or Orthodox canons, but often omitted or separated in Protestant editions.</term>) is 73, additional middle positions have been assigned—five for the **Psalms of David**, and three each for **Proverbs**, **Sirach** <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus.</note>, and **Matthew**. Furthermore, as the 20th page contains four sayings, the total sum—including the frontispiece—amounts to 84 **copperplates** <term>Kupffer: illustrations printed from engraved copper sheets, a common method for high-quality book illustration in the 18th century.</term> found within this little volume.

->From the following table, however, it can be seen how many sayings from each book of Holy Scripture<-
->have been brought in and included here. Also, under which number each can be found.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the index of botanical illustrations to the "Third Index," which provides the full biblical text corresponding to the numbered emblems (figures) found throughout the book. This ensures continuity for the reader by connecting the symbolic "Figure-Sprüche" to their literal scriptural sources.</meta>

# Third Index of the Biblical Pictorial Sayings.

->Contains all the sayings included in this work, according to their complete and unadorned words;<-
->Which can serve as a convenient key for those who are not provided with a Bible, or do not know how to apply the correct and literal words to the figures <note>In emblem books of this period, "figures" refer to the symbolic copperplate engravings that accompany the text.</note>.<-

### On the Frontispiece,

As many as walk according to this rule, peace be on them. Galatians 6:16.
Write my commandment upon the tablet of your heart. Proverbs of Solomon 3:3.

### Leaf I.

Num. 1. The Lord says: Fear not, Abram, I am your shield and your exceedingly great reward. 1 Genesis 15:1.
2. Jesus Christ, the same yesterday and today, and also forever. Hebrews 13:8.
3. Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit who is in you, whom you have from God. 1 Corinthians 6:19.

### II.

4. The pillar of cloud never departed from the people of Israel by day, nor the pillar of fire by night. 2 Exodus 13:22.
5. When Moses went down from Mount Sinai, he had the two tablets of the testimony in his hand. 2 Exodus 34:29.
6. The Ark of the Covenant was overlaid everywhere with gold, in which was the golden jar <note>Literally "chest" or "box" in the German text.</note> that had the bread from heaven <note>Manna.</note>, the rod of Aaron, and the tablets of the covenant. Hebrews 9:4.

### III.

7. The priest shall slaughter the goat and bring its blood behind the curtain, and sprinkle it also in front of the mercy seat. 3 Leviticus 16:15.

<column-break/>

8. I am like a stray and lost sheep; seek Your servant, O Lord. Psalm 119:176.
9. I wash my hands in innocence and keep myself close to Your altar, O Lord. Psalm 26:6 <note>The original German text cites verse 28, likely a typographical error or a reference to a different psalter numbering; the text corresponds to Psalm 26:6.</note>.

### IV.

10. A star shall come out of Jacob, and a scepter shall rise out of Israel. 4 Numbers 24:17.
11. Christ says: How often I have wanted to gather your children together as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings. Matthew 23:37.
12. Behold, the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the Root of David, has prevailed to open the book and to break its seven seals. Revelation of John 5:5.

### V.

13. You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your strength. 5 Deuteronomy 6:5.
14. An ox knows its master, and a donkey its manger <note>The German text breaks off here at the word "seines" (its); the full verse from Isaiah 1:3 would conclude "its master's manger."</note>

15. I delight to do your will, my God, and your law is in my **heart**. Psalm 40 v. 9.

17. You, Lord, are my refuge, a strong **tower** against my enemies. Psalm 61 v. 4.
18. The wicked must become like chaff before the **wind**, and let the **angel** of the Lord drive them away. Psalm 35 v. 5.

19. Samson found a fresh **jawbone of an ass**, and reached out his **hand** and took it, and struck down a thousand men with it, and said: There they lie in **heaps**. Judges 15 v. 15.

->VIII.<-

22. The Lord make the **woman** who comes into your **house** like Rachel and Leah. Book of Ruth 4 v. 11.

Her **lamp** does not go out at night. She reaches out her **hand** to the distaff, and her fingers grasp the **spindle**. Proverbs 31 v. 10, 13, 18.
24. Your **wife** shall be like a fruitful **vine** <note>original: "Weinstock," literally "vineshaft" or "vinetree"</note> around your house, your **children** like olive **branches** around your **table**. Psalm 128 v. 3.

31. Solomon's sea-**ship** came once in three years, and brought **gold**, **silver**, **ivory**, **apes**, and **peacocks**. 1 Kings 10 v. 22.
32. Greed is a **root** of all evil. 1 Timothy 6 v. 10.
33. What does man have for his labor under the **sun**? The **eye** is never satisfied with seeing. The **ear** is never satisfied with hearing. And all is **vanity**. Ecclesiastes 1.

->XII.<-

34. To King Sennacherib the Lord speaks: I will put a ring in your **nose** and a **bit** in your **mouth**. 2 Kings 19 v. 28.
35. I lie with my soul among **lions**; the children of men are **flames**, their **teeth** are **spears** and **arrows**, and their tongues are sharp **swords**. Psalm 57 v. 5.
36. He who takes the **sword** shall perish by the **sword**. Matthew 26 v. 52.

38. My **eyes** look always toward the Lord, for he will pluck my foot out of the **net**. Psalm 25 v. 15.

39. He who planted the **ear**, should he not hear? He who made the **eye**, should he not see? Psalm 94 v. 9.

44. Praise the Lord all his **angels**, praise him all his host. Praise him **sun** and **moon**, praise him all bright **stars**. Psalm 148 v. 2. 3.
45. The Lord is God, who enlightens us; decorate the feast with **may-branches** <note>original: "Meyen," referring to fresh green boughs used for festival decorations</note> even to the **horns** of the **altar**. Psalm 118 v. 27.

46. With one **hand** they built the **walls** of Jerusalem, and in the other they held **spear**, **shield**, and **bow**, and each had his **sword** girded at his loins. Nehemiah 4 v. 16. 17. 18.
47. God makes my **feet** like those of the **deer** <note>original: "Hirschen," often translated as hinds or stags</note>, and sets me upon my high places. Psalm 18 v. 34.
48. Our soul waits for the Lord; He is our help and **shield**. Psalm 33 v. 20.

->XVII.<-

49. The **king** saw **Queen** Esther, and she found favor in his **eyes**, and he stretched out the golden **scepter** in his **hand** toward her. Esther 5 v. 2.
50. Lord, my **heart** is not haughty, and my **eyes** are not proud. Psalm 131 v. 1.
51. **Flutes** <note>original: "Pfeiffen"</note> and **harps** sound good, but a friendly word is better than both. Sirach 40 v. 21.

<column-break/>

->XVIII.<-

52. The hope of the hypocrites is a spider's web. Job 8 v. 14.
53. If you crushed the **fool** in a **mortar** with a **pestle** <note>original: "Stempffel"</note> like grain, yet his foolishness would not leave him. Proverbs 27 v. 22.
54. A rogue <note>original: "Schalck"</note> can hang his **head**, he casts down his **eyes**, and listens with a rogue's **ears**, and yet it is vain deceit. Sirach 19 v. 23. 24.

->XIX.<-

55. My **shield** is with God, who helps those with upright **hearts**. Psalm 7 v. 11.
56. The righteous will shine like the **sun** in their Father's kingdom. Matthew 13 v. 43.
57. While God waited once in the times of Noah, the **ark** was prepared, in which few—that is, eight souls—were saved through the **water**. 1 Peter 3 v. 20.

59. A troubled and broken **heart** you, O GOD, will not despise. Psalm 51 v. 19.

64. Set me like a **seal** upon your **heart**, and like a seal upon your **arm**. Song of Solomon 8 v. 6.

->XXII.<-

65. Lift up your **gates**, and be lifted up, you everlasting **doors**, that the **King** of Glory may enter. Psalm 24 v. 7.

67. My friend is like a **roe** or a young **hart**. Behold, he stands behind our **wall**, and looks through the **window**, and peers through the **lattice**. Song of Solomon 2 v. 9.

->XXIII.<-

68. Let the Lord cause kindness and faithfulness to meet one another, and **righteousness** and **peace** to kiss. Psalm 85 v. 11. <note>In many English Bibles, this is verse 10.</note>
69. Better is a dish of **herbs** with love, than a fatted **ox** with hatred. Proverbs 15 v. 17.
70. You have seen, says the Lord, how I have carried you on **eagles' wings**, and have brought you unto myself. Exodus 19 v. 4.

->XXIV.<-

71. The Lord hates haughty **eyes**, lying **tongues**, **hands** that shed innocent blood, a **heart** that devises wicked schemes, and **feet** that are swift to do harm. Proverbs 6 v. 17. 18.
72. If a man desires to argue with God, he cannot answer Him **one** time in a **thousand**. Job 9 v. 3.
73. A **stork** under the **heaven** knows her time, a **turtle-dove**, **crane**, and **swallow** observe their time, etc. But my people do not want to know the law of the Lord. Jeremiah 8 v. 7.

->XXV.<-

74. The **name** of the Lord is a strong **castle** <note>original: "Schloß," often translated as tower or fortress</note>. Proverbs 18 v. 10.
75. Lord, by your favor you have made my **mountain** stand strong: but when you hid your **face**, I was terrified. Psalm 30 v. 8.
76. Whoever hears the words of Christ and does them is like a **man** who builds his **house** upon a **rock**. Matthew 7 v. 24.

->XXVI.<-

77. My son, give me your **heart**, and let your **eyes** take delight in my ways. Proverbs 23 v. 26.

<column-break/>

78. Create in me, O God, a clean **heart**, and give me a new, steadfast spirit. Psalm 51 v. 12.
79. Lord, you made the **moon** to mark the seasons <note>original: "das Jahr darnach zu theilen," literally "to divide the year accordingly"</note>, and the **sun** knows its setting. Psalm 104 v. 19.

->XXVII.<-

80. Remember your Creator in your youth, before the **sun** and the **light**, the **moon** and the **stars** grow dark. Ecclesiastes 12 v. 1. 2.
81. Be not like **horses** and **mules**, which have no understanding, whose mouths must be held with **bridle** and **bit**. Psalm 32 v. 9.
82. God said to the fallen Adam: Cursed be the **field** because of you, **thorns** and **thistles** shall it bear for you, and in the sweat of your **face** you shall eat your **bread**. Genesis 3 v. 17. 18. 19.

->XXVIII.<-

83. Let my friend come into his **garden**, and eat of his noble **fruits**. Song of Solomon 5 v. 1.
84. I am a **rose** <note>original: "Rose." Luther’s German translation uses "Rose," whereas English versions like the KJV traditionally use "Lily of the valley."</note> in the valley, like a **rose** among the **thorns**. Song of Solomon 2 v. 2.
85. He refreshes me with **flowers**, and revives me with **apples**, for I am sick with love. Song of Solomon 2 v. 5.

->XXIX.<-

86. In the Lord I have **righteousness** and **strength**. Isaiah 45 v. 24.
87. God has given us **rain** from **heaven** and fruitful seasons, filling our **hearts** with food and gladness. Acts 14 v. 17.
88. The righteous shall flourish like a **palm tree** and grow like a **cedar** in Lebanon. Psalm 92 v. 13.

->XXX.<-

89. **Death** has climbed in through our **windows** and entered into our palaces. Jeremiah 9 v. 21.

91. As for man, his life is like **grass**; he flourishes like a **flower** of the field; when the **wind** passes over it, it is gone. Psalm 103 v. 15. 16.

->XXXI.<-

92. The crown of our **head** is fallen: O **woe** that we have so sinned. Our **heart** is troubled, and our **eyes** have become dim. Lamentations 5 v. 16, 17.

->XXXIV.<-

101. It shall go ill for all inhabitants, for even the **beasts** of the field, the **birds** under **heaven**, and the **fish in the sea** shall be snatched away. Hosea 4 v. 3.
102. Assyria is the **rod** of my anger, and their **hand** is the **staff** of my wrath; may an **axe** <note>original: "Art," an archaic form for axe</note> boast against him who hews with it, or a **saw** defy him who pulls it? Isaiah 10 v. 5, 15.
103. And a **smoke** went out of the **well** <note>original: "Brunnen," here referring to the bottomless pit</note>, like that of a great **furnace**, and the **sun** was darkened, and there came **locusts** and **scorpions** upon the earth. Revelation of John 9 v. 2, 3.

104. The **vine** stands miserably; the **fig tree**, **pomegranate**

**trees**, **palm trees**, **apple trees**, and all the **trees** of the field are withered. Joel 1 v. 12.
105. If one will not be converted, then God has whetted his **sword**, and bent his **bow**, and aimed; he has prepared his **arrows** for destruction. Psalm 7 v. 13, 14.
106. Can one also gather **grapes** from **thorns**, or **figs** from **thistles**? Matthew 7 v. 16.

->XXXVI.<-

107. It is the Lord who makes the **mountains**, creates the **wind**, and shows to man what he should speak. Amos 4 v. 13.
108. One will fear you as long as the **sun** and the **moon** endure, from generation to generation. Psalm 72 v. 5.
109. God has put everything under man's **feet**, **sheep** and **oxen** all together, also the wild **beasts**, the **birds** under **heaven**, and the **fish** in the sea. Psalm 8 v. 8, 9.

->XXXVII.<-

110. The pride of your **heart** has deceived you, because you dwell in the **clefts of the rocks**, in your high **castles** <note>original: "Schlössern," suggesting fortified dwellings or strongholds</note>. Obadiah v. 3.
111. The imagination and striving of the human **heart** is evil from youth upward. 1st Book of Moses <note>Genesis</note> 8 v. 21.
112. Wash your **heart** from wickedness, so that you may be helped. Jeremiah 4 v. 14.

->XXXVIII.<-

113. Jonah rejoiced greatly over the **gourd** <note>original: "Kürbis," refers to the plant God provided for Jonah's shade</note>, but the Lord created a **worm** that bit the **gourd**, so that it withered by the **sun** and a parching **east wind**. Jonah 4 v. 6, 7.
114. God, you are my helper, and under the **shadow** of your **wings** I rejoice. Psalm 63 v. 8.
115. The Lord has commanded his **angels** concerning you, that they protect you in all your ways, that they carry you in their **hands**, and you do not strike your **foot** against a **stone**. Psalm 91 v. 11, 12.

122. Behold, whoever is headstrong will have no rest in his **heart**, for the righteous lives by his **faith**. Habakkuk 2 v. 4.
123. All the words of **God** are purified and are a **shield** to those who trust in Him. Proverbs 30 v. 5.
124. Neither do people light a **lamp** and put it under a **bushel**, but on a **lampstand**, so that it gives light to all who are in the **house**: So let your **light** shine before people. Matthew 5 v. 15, 16.

125. All the **world**, says the Lord, shall be consumed by the **fire** of my zeal. Zephaniah 3 v. 8.
126. I said: O, if only I had **wings** like **doves**, that I might fly away and remain somewhere. Psalm 55 v. 7.

131. Let none of you think any evil in his **heart** against his neighbor. Zechariah 8 v. 17.
132. The **fool’s** gift will not profit you much, for he gives with one **eye**, and with seven **eyes** he looks for what he might get in return. Sirach 20 v. 13. <note>In this context, "giving with one eye" refers to a stingy or insincere gift given while already looking for a reward.</note>
133. An **eye** that mocks its father and scorns to obey its mother—the **ravens** by the brook must pick it out, and the young **eagles** must eat it. Proverbs 30 v. 17.

134. Soon the Lord whom you seek shall come to His **temple**, even the **messenger** <note>original: "Engel," literally angel, but often translated as messenger in this context</note> of the covenant whom you desire. Malachi 3 v. 1.
135. In the **Tabernacle** was the **lampstand**, the **table**, and the **showbread**. Hebrews 9 v. 2.

137. Judith braided her **hair** to deceive Holofernes; her beautiful **shoes** dazzled him, and her beauty captured his **heart**, but she struck off his **head**. Judith 16 v. 10, 11.

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of biblical excerpts used as symbolic or moral instruction. The previous page ended at verse 142 within section XLIII. This page begins with section XLVIII, though there appears to be a slight irregularity in the numbering of the section headings (jumping from XLVIII to XLIV before continuing to L).</meta>

### XLVIII.

143. Throughout your life, keep God before your **eyes** and in your **heart**, and guard yourself so that you do not consent to any sin. Tobit 4 v. 6.
144. Whoever loves his **child** keeps him constantly under the **rod**, so that he may afterwards experience joy in him. Sirach 30 v. 1. <note>Sirach is also known as Ecclesiasticus; these verses reflect historical parenting advice regarding discipline.</note>
145. Folly is lodged in the **heart** of a boy, but the **rod** of discipline will drive it far from him. Proverbs of Solomon 22 v. 15.

### XLIV.

<note>The Roman numeral here appears out of sequence in the original manuscript, following XLVIII.</note>
146. The fear of the Lord is honor and glory, joy and a beautiful **crown**. Sirach 1 v. 10.
147. Let your **ear** pay attention to wisdom, and incline your **heart** diligently toward it. Proverbs of Solomon 2 v. 2.
148. **Prudence** is a living **fountain** to the one who possesses it. Proverbs of Solomon 16 v. 22.

### L.

149. Everything comes from God. **Fortune** and **misfortune**, life and **death**. Sirach 11 v. 14.
150. Guard me, Lord, as the **apple** of the **eye**; protect me under the shadow of your **wings**. Psalm 17 v. 8.
151. But yet I said, I must suffer this; the right **hand** of the Lord can change everything. Psalm 77 v. 11.

### LI.

152. Flee from sin as from a **snake**. Its **teeth** are like **lion's teeth**. Every sin is like a sharp **sword**, and it wounds so that no one can heal it. Sirach 21 v. 2, 3, 4.
153. A **whip** for the horse, a **bridle** for the donkey, and a **rod** for the **fool's** back. Proverbs of Solomon 26 v. 3.
154. The **fool's heart** is like a **wheel** on a **wagon**, and his thoughts run in circles. Sirach 33 v. 5. <note>The original citation lists Sirach 35, but this metaphor is found in chapter 33.</note>

### LII.

155. Night-**owls** and other **birds** sit upon the **heads** of their idols; likewise, the **cats** do the same. Baruch 6 v. 21. <note>This refers to the Letter of Jeremiah, often included as Baruch chapter 6, mocking the helplessness of man-made idols.</note>
156. When a word is stuck in a **fool**, it is just as if an **arrow** were stuck in his thigh. Sirach 19 v. 12.

<column-break/>

157. These people do not see with their **eyes**, nor hear with their **ears**, nor understand with their **heart**; they do not convert, and they do not recover. Isaiah 6 v. 10.

### LIII.

158. Every **elephant** carried a wooden **tower**, in which were **thirty-two warriors**, and the **Indian**, who guided the beasts. 1 Maccabees 6 v. 37. <note>original: "Mohr" (Moor). In historical German Bible translations, this term was used for the mahouts or elephant drivers, often of African or Indian origin.</note>
159. Here is the **sword** of the Lord and of Gideon. Book of Judges 7 v. 20.
160. The Lord teaches my **hand** to fight and teaches my **arm** to bend a **bow** of bronze. Psalm 18 v. 35.

### LIV.

161. The Maccabees cleansed the **temple** and made another **altar**, lit the **lamps**, and set out the **showbread**. 2 Maccabees 10 v. 3. <note>Showbread: twelve loaves of bread placed weekly on a golden table in the Sanctuary as an offering.</note>
162. As a **hart** cries out for fresh **water**, so my soul cries out to you, O God. Psalm 42 v. 2.
163. Is my word not like a **fire**, says the Lord, and like a **hammer** that shatters the **rocks**? Jeremiah 23 v. 29.

### LV.

164. The **axe** is already laid to the **root** of the **trees**. Therefore, every **tree** that does not bring forth good **fruit** is cut down and thrown into the **fire**. Matthew 3 v. 10.
165. A man does not know his time; rather, just as **fish** are caught with a harmful **net** and **birds** with a **snare**, so too are people ensnared. Ecclesiastes 9 v. 12.
166. Though you polish your enemy as you would a **mirror**, he will nevertheless remain rusty. Sirach 12 v. 11.

### LVI.

167. You shall not give that which is holy to the **dogs**, and you shall not cast your **pearls** before **swine**. Matthew 7 v. 6.
168. If you are not righteous, then sin rests before the **door**. Genesis 4 v. 7.
169. Woe to you who make **cushions** for people's **arms** and **pillows** for their **heads**. Ezekiel 13 v. 18. <note>The original citation lists Ezekiel 33, but this condemnation of false prophetesses is found in chapter 13.</note>

Bible verses, sayings, virtues, vices, metaphors, animals, tools

->LVII.<-

170. For out of the abundance of the **heart**, the **mouth** speaks. Matthew 12 v. 34.
171. One who speaks thoughtlessly pierces like a **sword**. Proverbs of Solomon 14 v. 18. <note>The verse citation in the original refers to Proverbs; in most modern Bibles, this sentiment is found in Proverbs 12:18.</note>
172. Guard the doors of your **mouth** from her who sleeps in your arms. Micah 7 v. 5.

->LVIII.<-

173. It is easier for a **camel** to go through the eye of a **needle** than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God. Mark 10 v. 25.
174. A living **dog** is better than a dead **lion**. Ecclesiastes 9 v. 4. <note>original: "Pred. Sal." (Preacher Solomon), the German name for the Book of Ecclesiastes.</note>
175. Some are only friends at the **table**, and will not stand by you in time of need. Sirach 6 v. 10. <note>Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus, is a book of the Apocrypha frequently quoted in this period for its practical wisdom.</note>

->LIX.<-

176. No one who puts his **hand** to the **plow** and looks back is fit for the kingdom of God. Luke 9 v. 62.
177. A slack **hand** makes one poor, but the **hand** of the diligent makes one rich. Proverbs of Solomon 10 v. 4.
178. Can a **Moor** change his skin, or a **leopard** his spots? Then you also can do good, who are accustomed to doing evil. Jeremiah 13 v. 23. <note>original: "Mohr" (Moor) and "Pardel" (Leopard). This verse describes the difficulty of changing one's nature.</note>

->LX.<-

179. Behold, this is the **Lamb** of God, who bears the sin of the world. John 1 v. 29.
180. Christ says: Whoever does not bear his **cross** and follow me cannot be my disciple. Luke 14 v. 7. <note>The original cites verse 7, though this teaching is typically found in Luke 14:27.</note>
181. The Lord Messiah speaks: **Dogs** have surrounded me, and a pack of wicked men has closed in on me. They have pierced my **hands** and **feet**; I could count all my bones. Psalm 22 v. 17.

->LXI.<-

182. Behold, the **angel** of the Lord appeared, and a **light** shone in the room; he struck Peter on the side, and the **chains** fell from his hands. Acts of the Apostles 12 v. 7.
183. A king’s **wrath** is a messenger of **death**. Proverbs of Solomon 16 v. 14.
184. The **death** of his saints is held in high esteem before the Lord. Psalm 116 v. 15.

->LXII.<-

185. **Death** is the wages of sin. Romans 6 v. 4. <note>The text likely intends to cite Romans 6:23, which contains this famous phrase.</note>
186. Lord my God, enlighten my **eyes**, so that I do not fall asleep in **death**. Psalm 13 v. 4.
187. **Death** is better than a sickly life or constant illness. Sirach 30 v. 17.

<column-break/>

->LXIII.<-

188. Just as we have borne the **image** of the earthly, so shall we also bear the **image** of the heavenly. 1 Corinthians 15 v. 49.
189. The kingdom of God is **righteousness** and **peace** and **joy** in the Holy Spirit. Romans 14 v. 17.
190. You have ravished my **heart**, my sister, my dear bride, with one of your **eyes** and with one chain of your **necklace**. Song of Solomon 4 v. 9. <note>original: "Hohel. Sal." (High Song of Solomon).</note>

->LXIV.<-

191. Do not pull at a strange **yoke** with the unbelieving. 2 Corinthians 6 v. 14.
192. The wicked man lurks in secret, like a **lion** in his den. Psalm 10 v. 9.
193. Possess your **soul** with patience. Luke 21 v. 19.

->LXV.<-

194. But far be it from me to boast, except in the **cross** of our Lord Jesus Christ, by which the world is crucified to me, and I to the world. Galatians 6 v. 14.
195. Be wise as **serpents** and as innocent as **doves**. Matthew 10 v. 16.
196. Lord, with you is the living **fountain**, and in your **light** we see the **light**. Psalm 36 v. 10.

->LXVI.<-

197. Put on the **armor** of God. Take up the **shield** of faith, with which you can extinguish all the fiery arrows of the wicked one. Take the **helmet** of salvation and the **sword** of the Spirit. Ephesians 6.
198. Let not a **strong man** boast of his **strength**. Jeremiah 9 v. 23.
199. They are pregnant with misfortune and give birth to trouble; they hatch **basilisk** eggs and weave **spider webs**. If one steps on them, an **adder** comes out. Isaiah 59 v. 4, 5. <note>A "basilisk" or "cockatrice" was a mythical serpent-king said to kill with its glance.</note>

->LXVII.<-

200. Jesus Christ humbled himself and was obedient even unto **death** on the **cross**. Philippians 2 v. 8.
201. Before the **mountains** were brought forth, and the earth and the world were created, you are God from eternity to eternity. Psalm 90 v. 2.
202. The righteous and pious man is a despised **little light** in the thoughts of the proud. Job 12 v. 5.

205. I lift my **eyes** up to the mountains, from which my help comes. Psalm 121 v. 1.

206. We remember your work in **faith**, and your labor in **love**, and your **patience** in **hope**. 1 Thessalonians 1 v. 3.
207. A word spoken at its proper time is like golden **apples** in silver **bowls**. Proverbs of Solomon 25 v. 11.
208. Oh, that I could set a **lock** on my **mouth**, and press a firm **seal** upon my **lips**, so that I would not fall because of them, and my tongue would not destroy me. Sirach 23 v. 1. <note>The book of Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus, provides practical moral advice; the original German uses "Maul" for the second mention of the mouth, a more blunt term often used for animals, emphasizing the need for restraint.</note>

209. The Lord Jesus will be revealed from **heaven** together with the **angels** of his power, and with **flaming fire**, to take vengeance upon those who do not recognize God. 2 Thessalonians 1 v. 7–8.
210. Those who love the Lord must be like the **sun** rising in its power. Book of Judges 5 v. 31.
211. God, your **throne** remains forever and ever; the **scepter** of your kingdom is a righteous **scepter**. Psalm 45 v. 7. <note>original: "Stuhl" (literally "chair" or "seat," used here to mean a throne).</note>

212. The main sum of the commandment is **love** from a pure **heart**. 1 Timothy 1 v. 5.
213. Just as when you let a **bird** out of your **hand**, so it is when you abandon your friend. Sirach 27 v. 21.
214. A false **scale** is an abomination to the Lord, but a full **weight** is his delight. Proverbs of Solomon 11 v. 1.

215. I have kept the faith; henceforth the **crown** of righteousness is laid up for me, which the Lord will give to me. 2 Timothy 4 v. 7–8.

216. I would rather guard the **door** in the house of my God than dwell long in the **tents** of the godless: For God the Lord is **sun** and **shield**: The Lord gives grace and honor. Psalm 84 v. 11–12.
217. Guard your **heart** with all diligence, for from it flows life. Proverbs of Solomon 4 v. 23.

->LXXIII.<- <note>The original OCR reads "LXIII," which is likely a printer's error for "LXXIII" (73) following "LXXII" (72).</note>

218. A **bishop** shall be blameless. Titus 1 v. 7.
219. The priest’s lips should preserve the teaching, so that one seeks the **law** from his mouth, for he is an **angel** of the Lord of Hosts. Malachi 2 v. 7. <note>In this context, "angel" refers to the role of a messenger or herald of God.</note>
220. Do not let the **book** of this law depart from your **mouth**. Joshua 1 v. 8.

221. I thank my God, as I hear of the **love** and the **faith** which you have toward the Lord JESUS. Philemon v. 4–5.

223. Do not fan the **fire** of the godless, so that you do not also burn with it. Sirach 8 v. 13.

228. Like **smoke** to the **eyes**, so is the lazy man to those who send him. Proverbs of Solomon 10 v. 26.

229. Which of you, if his **ox** or **donkey** falls into a **well**, does not immediately pull him out? Luke 14 v. 5.

->LXXVII.<-

230. Our **faith** is the victory that has overcome the **world**. 1 John 5 v. 4.
231. The shepherds came in haste and found both **Mary** and **Joseph**, as well as the **Child JESUS** lying in the manger. Luke 2 v. 16.
232. Wisdom is more precious than **pearls**, and all that you may desire is not to be compared to it. Proverbs of Solomon 3 v. 15.

->LXXVIII.<-

233. Many deceivers have come into the **world**, who do not confess **JESUS Christ**, that he has come in the flesh. 2 John v. 7.
234. Where does a son ask the father for **bread**, and he offers him a **stone**, or for a **fish**, and he offers him a **snake**, or for an **egg**, and he offers him a **scorpion**? Luke 11 v. 11–12.
235. Do not rely on lies, for they say: Here is the **temple** of the Lord, etc. Instead, improve your life and being. Jeremiah 7 v. 4–5.

->LXXIX.<-

236. I have no greater joy than this, that I hear my **children** walking in the truth. 3 John v. 4.
237. Jacob saw a **ladder** that stood upon the earth and touched the **heavens** with its **top**, and behold, the **angels of God** were ascending and descending on it. Genesis <note>original: "1. Buch Mos." (1st Book of Moses)</note> 28 v. 12.
238. Look at the **rainbow**, and praise him who made it. Sirach 43 v. 2. <note>Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus, is a book of the Apocrypha; in modern English editions, this verse is found in Sirach 43:11.</note>

->LXXX.<-

239. We hold fast to the offered **hope**, which we have as a sure and firm **anchor** of our souls. Hebrews 5 v. 18–19. <note>In most English Bibles, this is Hebrews 6:18–19.</note>
240. **Righteousness** was my garment, which I put on like a **robe**, and my justice was my princely **hat**. I was **eyes** to the blind and **feet** to the lame. Job 29 v. 14. <note>The author combines Job 29:14–15.</note>
241. I do not rely on my **bow**, and my **sword** cannot help me. Psalm 44 v. 7. <note>In many English Bibles, this is Psalm 44:6.</note>

<column-break/>

->LXXXI.<-

242. If anyone is a hearer of the word and not a doer, he is like a **man** who looks at his physical **face** in a **mirror** and forgets what he looked like. James 1 v. 23–24.
243. The **light** is sweet, and it is pleasant for the **eyes** to see the **sun**. Ecclesiastes <note>original: "Pred. Sal." (Preacher Solomon)</note> 11 v. 7.
244. A joyful **heart** makes a joyful **face**. Proverbs of Solomon 15 v. 13.

->LXXXII.<-

245. The ungodly are **clouds** without water, driven about by the **wind**, bare unfruitful **trees** twice dead and uprooted, wild **waves** of the sea, wandering **stars**. Epistle of Jude v. 12–13.
246. **Fools** have their **heart** in their **mouth**, but the wise have their **mouth** in their **heart**. Sirach 21 v. 28. <note>In modern editions, this is Sirach 21:26.</note>
247. At that time, the Lord will take away the finery of the daughter of Zion: the precious **shoes**, the **chains**, the **bracelets**, the **bonnets**, the **rings**, the **hair-bands**, the **cloaks**, the **veils**, the **mirrors**, the **ear-spangles**, the **purses**, the **braids**, and instead he will give a narrow **sack**. Isaiah 3 v. 18 to 24.

->LXXXIII.<-

248. Be faithful unto **death**, and I will give you the **crown** of life. Revelation of John Chapter 2 v. 10.
249. The Lord **JESUS** will come in his glory, and all the holy **angels** with him, and he will place the **sheep** at his right hand and the **goats** at the left. Matthew 25 v. 31–32.
250. The grace of our Lord **JESUS** Christ, the love of **GOD** the **Father**, and the fellowship of the **Holy Spirit** be with you all. 2 Corinthians 13 v. 13. <note>In most English translations, this is 2 Corinthians 13:14.</note>

# A M E N.

->Seek, my friend, only God’s honor,<-
->And turn this work to the best end.<-

# Spiritual Imaginings of the Heart
### In Two Hundred and Fifty
## Biblical Figurative Proverbs
### Indicated.

To all devout hearts and youth who love virtue,
for a godly amusement,
also for the simple-minded
for a charming presentation,
for easy grasping and useful comprehension,
from all canonical books of the Holy Scripture
newly issued in a charming manner with beautiful letters,
well-arranged figures, and neat copperplates,
by
**Johann Simon Negges**
Copperplate Engraver and Art Publisher
in Augsburg.

---

<margin>Top left</margin>
As many as walk according to this <note>original: "Regel" (rule/standard). Refers to Galatians 6:16: "And as many as walk according to this rule, peace be on them, and mercy."</note>

<margin>Middle left</margin>
peace be upon them

<margin>Bottom left</margin>
Galatians 6:16

The page is framed by devotional verses emphasizing that the Word of God serves as spiritual food, light, and protection.</summary>

<image-desc>An ornate, hand-colored engraved title page. The central text is surrounded by ten heart-shaped vignettes, each containing a symbolic emblem (e.g., a pomegranate, a sun, an eye, a hand holding a key, a skull). Two female allegorical figures stand on the left and right sides upon pedestals. The figure on the left holds a heart over a flame; the figure on the right writes upon a tablet. At the top center, the Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, Yahweh</note> shines within a sunburst. The entire composition is framed by architectural elements and floral garlands.</image-desc>

->The [tablets] The [earrings] The [rings]<-
->The buckles, the [nose jewels]<- <note>The rebus uses repeated "The" (Die) prompts to point to the various illustrations of feminine luxury mentioned in the biblical text.</note>
->and will give in its place a<-
->narrow [sack]<- <note>original: "engen Sack." This refers to sackcloth, a symbol of mourning and humiliation that replaces fine clothing.</note>

For the baggage of prideful splendor,
God often gives a narrow sack.

<summary>This page features three biblical rebuses illustrating the folly of loose speech, the spiritual emptiness of "freethinkers," and the divine punishment of vanity and pride.</summary>

### Of those five hundred
# <term>Spiritual Conceptions of the Heart</term>, <note>original: "Geistlichen Herßens-Einbildungen." In the context of 18th-century devotion, "Einbildungen" refers to "imaginations" or "inner representations"—symbolic images meant to be visualized in the heart during prayer.</note>
## Second Part.
### In two hundred and fifty
# <term>Biblical Pictorial-Verses</term> <note>original: "Biblischen Figur-Sprüchen." This refers to emblems or "figure-poems" where a scriptural passage is paired with a symbolic illustration to deepen the reader's understanding.</note>
### presented.

---

->To be found at<-
### Johann Simon Negges, in <term>Augsburg</term> <note>original: "Augspurg." A prominent imperial city in Germany, famous at the time for its high-quality engraving and printing houses.</note>.

<summary>This is the title page for the second part of a devotional emblem book, featuring 250 biblical illustrations meant for spiritual meditation. It was published in the major printing center of Augsburg by Johann Simon Negges.</summary>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page serves as a poetic preface or "introductory greeting" to the second volume. It establishes a month-by-month guide for using the emblem book as a devotional tool throughout the calendar year, connecting the first part (the senses) to this new part (the heart's renewal).</meta>

After the first part has initiated the hearts,
The drive and desire are now renewed through the second;
God’s faithful heart draws powerfully like a magnet
The heart that does not stubbornly resist the pull.
Rather, the Sun allows the darkness to be transfigured
And shows itself ready to follow upon hearing,
Also gladly bending into the yoke of the Highest’s will
And accepting with patience what His wisdom ordains.
It was shown before how pleasant within this
Utility and amusement are found for the five senses;
Now follows here the report of how every month-time
Finds a special usefulness from this contemplation.
When in <term>January</term> <note>original: "Jenner."</note> one must remain shut indoors,
One can pass the long hours quite sweetly with this;
In <term>February</term> <note>original: "Hornung."</note> the world seeks forbidden Carnival-joys,
A pious heart finds here the true delight for the eyes.
The <term>March-Moon</term> delights us through nature’s renewal,
Here constant springtime-joy refreshes for the <term>soul’s prosperity</term> <note>original: "Seel-Gedeyen."</note>.
Though the weather changes suddenly in April,
The <term>Sun of Grace</term> <note>original: "Gnaden-Sonn." A metaphor for divine favor or Christ.</note> wants to shine upon us here always.
The world is accustomed to prize May’s charm very highly,
Yet it does not reach the delight of this work.
If the <term>fallow month’s</term> <note>original: "Brachmonat," an old agricultural name for June.</note> heat makes many thirsty and sick,
Here the cooling refreshing-drink reveals itself in <term>Baptism</term>.

<column-break/>

Even if the <term>hay month</term> <note>original: "Heumonat," an old name for July.</note> should offer ever so many fruits,
This treasure will never yield to its great gifts.
Just as August provides bread for sustenance,
So here the <term>Bread of Life</term> <note>original: "Lebens-Brod."</note> gives comfort and strength to the soul.
If the <term>harvest month</term> <note>original: "Herbstmonat," an old name for September.</note> mildly wants to pour out the sweet must,
O, this cup shall quench our thirst in a far more healing way.
With this noble liquid the <term>wine month</term> <note>original: "Weinmond," an old name for October.</note> endows us;
Well-off is he whom the heavenly nectar refreshes in his soul.
The cold <term>winter month</term> <note>original: "Wintermond," an old name for November.</note> teaches us to take up the hunt;
Wise is he who strives to hunt after the <term>scriptural verses</term>.
In the <term>Christ-month</term> <note>original: "Christmond," the traditional German name for December.</note> one shuts oneself in warm rooms;
O, well-off is he who can be warm with Jesus’s love!
Thus one can well put the whole year behind them
When one wants to rightly weigh the images in the spirit;
In this contemplation, no day will ever be too long for us.
It cheers the spirit whenever we start to feel anxious.
The tender swarm of children is thus accustomed in time,
So that they long more and more for the heavenly,
And in old age the effect first comes to pass
From that which was learned here as a child.
May God then bless His work, devised for His honor,
And grant that many a heart be won thus with joy;
As His image and word delight it in this <term>valley of sorrows</term> <note>original: "Jammerthal." A common religious term for earthly life.</note>,
So may the Original <note>Meaning God Himself, whom the symbolic images represent.</note> bring him eternal joy there.

<summary>This poetic preface introduces the second volume of the emblem book, presenting it as a year-round spiritual companion. It provides a monthly guide where natural seasonal events are transformed into spiritual lessons, ultimately aiming to train the reader—from childhood to old age—for eternity.</summary>
<keywords>preface, devotional poetry, liturgical year, months, spiritual growth, baptism, bread of life, religious education, emblems</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous introductory poem, moving from the poetic dedication of the months into the formal publisher's preface. It transitions from the calendar-based usage guide to a justification for the book's existence and a description of its physical layout.</meta>

->To the<-
# favorable and gracious reader and beholder
->of these<-
## Biblical Figure-Sayings.
### Publisher’s Preface.

Although the precious Word of God is such an incomparable <term>food for the soul</term> <note>original: "Seelen-Speiß."</note> and heavenly manna that no distaste should arise toward it in human minds; yet sorrowful experience teaches that for many, the appetite often—if not vanishing entirely—is very delicate and choosy. To prevent or remedy this spiritual malady, many men learned in God’s Word and Christians zealous for the appreciation of this high treasure of the soul have applied their God-given talents so that the Word of God—according to the exhortation of Paul in <term>Colossians 3:16</term> <note>original: "Coloss. III. v. 16."</note>—might dwell among us not only richly, but also delightfully and agreeably in all wisdom. The singular <term>applause</term> <note>original: "Applausus," meaning general approval or acclaim.</note> and approval given to the first part has prompted the continuation of this praiseworthy work, and the decision to undertake its once again costly <term>publication</term> <note>original: "Verlag."</note>. This second part is uniform with the first in every way, so that across 83 leaves (except for folio 43, where four figures had to be placed), the main verse is always in the center

<column-break/>

<note>This column contains a list of key terms found in the text for reference: Biblical Figure-Sayings, Preface, Publisher, Soul-Food, Manna, Colossians 3, Applause, Publication.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces the second volume of "Biblical Figure-Sayings," explaining that the book uses illustrations and "agreeable" formatting to help readers who find plain scripture difficult to digest. The publisher notes that the success of the first volume led to this second part, which follows the same 83-page layout.</summary>
<keywords>devotional literature, emblem book, Bible study, Lutheran piety, publishing history, soul-food, Colossians 3:16</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page concludes the publisher's preface for the "Biblical Figure-Sayings." It transitions from the physical description of the book's layout to an explanation of its symbolic themes—such as the senses and elements—and concludes with a pedagogical justification for using such illustrations to teach children.</meta>

The verses are arranged according to the order of the Biblical books, and thus 250 verses are presented in such a manner as a **spiritual show-dish** <note>original: "geistliches Schau-Essen," referring to a decorative banquet display used here as a metaphor for visually appealing religious instruction.</note>. In this part, the hearts are presented internally with their **affections** <note>original: "Affecten," meaning passions, emotions, or character traits</note> or qualities, as far as has been possible. Also, to demonstrate what beautiful inventions can still be brought forth from Holy Scripture, the author has depicted the **five senses** from the five books of Moses under Nos. 3, 6, 9, 12, and 15; the **four elements** under No. 55; the **four Evangelists** under No. 76; and the **four seasons** under Nos. 127, 128, 129, and 130.

Just as it is indisputable that, wherever an improvement of Christianity is to be hoped for and undertaken, the beginning must be made with the good instruction of the youth; so the entire intention of this work is directed toward ensuring that dear children—who 

<column-break/>

are always eager to know something new—might be kept in the joy of learning through these **Figure-Sayings** <note>original: "Figur-Sprüche," verses accompanied by symbolic illustrations or emblems.</note>. Since they can simultaneously practice reading, observing, and reflecting in the most edifying way, **harmful idleness** <note>original: "schädliche Müßiggang," a common concern in 17th-century teaching, suggesting that bored children are prone to sin.</note> may be interrupted. Conversely, many beautiful verses may be rooted in their tender hearts as plants of salvation and stored up as a precious treasure for a time of need. The Lord will further bless His work through this means and lead the hearts of diligent parents to provide their dearly beloved youth with this little treatise, which is as charming as it is useful. It is also available from me **neatly illuminated** <note>original: "nett illuminiret," meaning the illustrations were hand-colored for a premium edition.</note> for the sake of greater elegance and a more natural representation. In this good hope, and wishing for every spiritual and bodily blessing, the publisher most highly commends himself and his publishing house with all due respect.

->The Publisher.<-

<note>
**Reference Vocabulary from this page:**
Biblical Books, Spiritual Show-Dish, Affections, Qualities, 5 Senses, 4 Elements, 4 Evangelists, 4 Seasons, Christianity, Youth, Figure-Sayings, Idleness, Little Treatise, Illuminated, Publisher.
</note>

<summary>This concluding preface explains the book's symbolic structure—incorporating the senses and elements into biblical teaching—and emphasizes its role in educating children through engaging visual emblems.</summary>
<keywords>religious education, emblem book, five senses, four elements, child pedagogy, publishing history, spiritual nourishment</keywords>

## Second Index
->regarding this second part of the Biblical Figure-Sayings.<-

->Contains all the sayings included in this work, according to their complete and unadorned words.<-

->Which can serve as a convenient key for those who are not provided with a Bible, or do not know how to place the right, actual words onto the figures <note>referring to the "Figures" or visual symbols in the illustrations</note>.<-

### On the Frontispiece.

By the image of Divine Grace: Give me, my son, your **heart**. Proverbs 23, verse 26.
By the image of the obedient heart: My **heart** is ready, God, my **heart** is ready. Psalm 57, verse 8.

Page I.
1. The Spirit of God hovered over the water. 1st Book of Moses <note>Genesis</note> 1, verse 2.
2. The law was given through Moses, but grace and truth came through Jesus Christ. John 1, verse 17.
3. Wisdom gives off a sweet smell like cinnamon and precious spices. Sirach <note>also known as Ecclesiasticus</note> 24, verse 20.

II.
4. The angel of the Lord appeared to Moses in a fiery flame from the bush, and he saw that the bush burned, and yet was not consumed. 2nd Book of Moses <note>Exodus</note> 3, verse 2.

<column-break/>

5. The main summary of the commandment is love from a pure heart, and from a good conscience, and from unfeigned faith. 1st Timothy 1, verse 5.
6. Gehazi laid the staff on the boy's face, but there was no voice nor feeling. 2nd Book of Kings 4, verse 31.

III.
7. You shall not hate your brother in your heart. 3rd Book of Moses <note>Leviticus</note> 19, verse 17.
8. Because you are then children, God has sent the Spirit of His Son into your hearts. Galatians 4, verse 6.
9. Lord God Father and Lord of my life, protect me from unchaste looks. Sirach 23, verses 4, 5.

IV.

<summary>This page provides a cross-reference index and the beginning of a literal transcription of the biblical verses used in the "Figure-Sayings" illustrations. It translates visual metaphors back into their original scriptural text for readers who may not have a Bible to reference.</summary>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Index" (B 2) of the biblical figure-sayings. It provides the full text of the scriptures that were represented visually on the preceding illustrated pages. The page is divided into two columns and organized by section numbers (IV through XII), corresponding to the layout of the emblem book.</meta>

->IV.<-

10. The **Lord** spoke to **Moses**: Make for yourself a bronze **serpent**, and set it up as a sign; whoever is bitten and looks at it shall live. Numbers <note>original: "4. B. Mos." - the Fourth Book of Moses</note> 21, verse 8.
11. **Christ** is the end of the law; whoever believes in him is righteous. Romans 10, verse 4.
12. The people ground the manna in **mortars**, and cooked it in **pots**, and it had a **smell** like oil-cakes. Numbers 11, verse 8.

->V.<-

13. Oh, says the **Lord**, that they had such a **heart** to fear me, and to keep all my **commandments** all their lives. Deuteronomy <note>original: "5. B. Mos." - the Fifth Book of Moses</note> 5, verse 29.
14. You were like wandering **sheep**: but you are now converted to the **Shepherd** and **Bishop** of your souls. 1 Peter 2, verse 25.
15. If the whole body were an **eye**, where then would be the **hearing**. 1 Corinthians 12, verse 17.

->VI.<-

16. So the priests carried the seven **trumpets** of the Jubilee year before the ark of the **Lord**, and went around the **city** of Jericho. Joshua 6, verse 13.
17. Praise God, O Zion, for he makes fast the bars of your **gates**, and blesses your **children** within. Psalm 147, verses 12, 13.
18. The **house** of the godless will be destroyed, but the **tent** of the righteous will flourish. Proverbs of Solomon <note>The text cites Proverbs 16:11, though this verse is typically found at Proverbs 14:11.</note> 16, verse 11.

->VII.<-

19. Adoni-Bezek spoke: **Seventy kings** with the **thumbs** of their hands and **feet** cut off gathered scraps under my **table**; as I have done, so has God requited me. Judges 1, verse 7.
20. It is a miserable, lamentable thing, concerning the **life** of all men, as well for him who sits in high **honors**, as for the **lowliest** on earth; as well for him who wears **silk** and a **crown**, as for him who has a coarse **smock** on. <note>This verse is a paraphrase from the Wisdom of Sirach 40:1-4.</note>
21. How has it come to pass, that the **hammer** of the whole **world** is broken and struck down. Jeremiah 50, verse 23.

<column-break/>

->VIII.<-

22. **Ruth**, the Moabitess, spoke to Naomi: Let me go into the **field** and glean **ears of grain** after him in whose sight I find grace. Ruth 2, verse 2.
23. A diligent **wife** is a **crown** to her **husband**. Proverbs of Solomon <note>The text cites verse 14, but this is commonly Proverbs 12:4.</note> 12, verse 14.
24. Such a **woman**, whose **heart** is **nets** and **snares**, and whose **hands** are bonds, is bitterer than **death**. Ecclesiastes <note>original: "Pred. Sal." - The Preacher Solomon</note> 7, verse 27.

->IX.<-

25. Only fear the Lord, and serve him faithfully, with all your **heart**, for you have seen what great things he does with you. 1 Samuel 12, verse 24.
26. Hannah prayed: My **heart** is joyful in the Lord, my **horn** is exalted, my **mouth** is opened wide over my enemies, for I rejoice in your **salvation**. 1 Samuel 2, verse 1.
27. David spoke to Abner: See, here is the **king's** **spear** and the **water-jug** that were at his **head**. 1 Samuel 26, verse 16.

->X.<-

28. Thus **David** became king over all Israel, and he established law and **justice** for all the people. 2 Samuel 8, verse 15.
29. The Lord wished to set a **man** over the congregation of the Lord, so that they would not be like **sheep** without a **shepherd**. Numbers 27, verses 16, 17.
30. A **man** is proven by the **mouth** of his praiser, as silver in the **crucible**, and gold in the **furnace**. Proverbs of Solomon 27, verse 21.

->XI.<-

31. May the Lord our God not forsake us, and not withdraw his **hand** from us, to incline our **heart** to him, that we may walk in all his ways. 1 Kings 8, verses 57, 58.
32. And **David** along with all Israel brought up the **ark** of the Lord with shouting and **trumpets**. 2 Samuel <note>The original text incorrectly cites 1 Samuel 6:15.</note> 6, verse 15.
33. **Wisdom** will beautifully adorn your **head**, and will decorate you with a lovely **crown**. Proverbs of Solomon 4, verse 9.

->XII.<-

34. The Lord opened the young man's **eyes**, so that he saw, and behold, the mountain was full of fiery **horses** and **chariots** around Elisha. 2 Kings 6, verse 17.

# Second Index
### concerning this second part of the Biblical Figure-Sayings.
->Contains within itself all the sayings included in this work, according to their complete and unclouded words.<-
->Which can serve as a convenient key for those who are not provided with a Bible, or do not know how to apply the right specific words to the figures.<-

### On the Frontispiece.
**By the image of Divine Grace.** Give me, my son, your **heart**. Prov. 23 v. 26.
**By the image of the obedient heart.** My **heart** is ready, God, my **heart** is ready. Ps. 57 v. 8.

#### Sheet I.
1. The Spirit of God moved upon the water. Genesis <note>original: "1. Buch. Mos." — the First Book of Moses</note> 1 v. 2.
2. The law was given through Moses, but grace and truth came through Jesus Christ. John 1 v. 17.
3. Wisdom gives off a sweet smell like cinnamon and precious spices. Sirach <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus</note> 24 v. 20.

#### II.
4. The angel of the Lord appeared to Moses in a fiery flame from the bush, and he saw that the bush burned, and yet was not consumed. Exodus <note>original: "2. B. Mos." — the Second Book of Moses</note> 3 v. 2.

<column-break/>

5. The sum total of the commandment is love from a pure heart, and from a good conscience, and from unfeigned faith. 1 Tim. 1 v. 5.
6. Gehazi laid the staff on the boy's face, but there was neither voice nor feeling. 2 Kings <note>original: "2. B. der König"</note> 4 v. 31.

#### III.
7. You shall not hate your brother in your heart. Leviticus <note>original: "3. B. Mos." — the Third Book of Moses</note> 19 v. 17.
8. Because you are then children, God has sent the Spirit of His Son into your hearts. Gal. 4 v. 6.
9. Lord God Father and Lord of my life, protect me from unchaste looks. Sirach 23 v. 4, 5.

#### IV.

<meta>This page continues the "Second Index," providing the full scriptural texts for the numbered emblems (Sheets IV through XII). The text follows the Lutheran Bible translation common in the 17th and 18th centuries. Each entry consists of a biblical verse where specific "keywords" (bolded here, originally in red ink) correspond to the central imagery of the associated emblem. There is a slight discrepancy in some verse numbering compared to modern Bibles, reflecting historical editions.</meta>

->IV.<-

10. The Lord spoke to **Moses**: Make for yourself a bronze **serpent**, and set it up as a sign; whoever is bitten and looks at it shall live. <note>Numbers 21:8</note>
11. **Christ** is the end of the law; whoever believes in him is righteous. <note>Romans 10:4</note>
12. The people ground the manna in **mortars**, and cooked it in **pots**, and it had a **smell** like oil-cakes. <note>Numbers 11:8</note>

->V.<-

13. Oh, says the Lord, that they had such a **heart** to fear me, and to keep all my **commandments** all their lives. <note>Deuteronomy 5:29</note>
14. For you were like straying **sheep**; but you are now converted to the **Shepherd** and **Bishop** <note>The term "Bischoff" here refers to an overseer or guardian</note> of your souls. <note>1 Peter 2:25</note>
15. If the whole body were an eye, where then would be the **hearing**. <note>1 Corinthians 12:17</note>

->VI.<-

16. So the priests carried the seven jubilee **trumpets** <note>original: "Hall-Jahrs Posaunen," referring to the ram's horns blown during the Year of Jubilee</note> before the Ark of the Lord, and went around the **city** of Jericho. <note>Joshua 6:13</note>
17. Praise your **God**, O Zion, for he makes fast the bars of your **gates**, and blesses your **children** within. <note>Psalm 147:12–13</note>
18. The **house** of the wicked will be destroyed, but the tent of the righteous will flourish. <note>Proverbs 14:11; the source cites chapter 16</note>

->VII.<-

19. Adoni-Bezek spoke: **Seventy kings** with their **thumbs** and **toes** of their **hands** and **feet** cut off gathered scraps under my **table**; as I have done, so God has repaid me. <note>Judges 1:7</note>
20. It is a miserable, lamentable thing concerning the **lives** of all men, as much for him who sits in high **honor** as for the lowliest on earth; as much for him who wears silk and a **crown**, as for him who wears a coarse **smock**. <note>This verse is from the Apocrypha, Sirach 40:1–4</note>
21. How has it happened, that the **hammer** of the whole **world** is broken and struck down. <note>Jeremiah 50:23, referring to the fall of Babylon</note>

<column-break/>

->VIII.<-

22. **Ruth**, the Moabite, spoke to Naomi: Let me go into the **field** and glean **ears of grain** after him in whose sight I find favor. <note>Ruth 2:2</note>
23. A diligent wife is a **crown** to her **husband**. <note>Proverbs 12:4; the source cites verse 14</note>
24. Such a **woman**, whose **heart** is **nets** and **snares**, and whose **hands** are bands, is more bitter than **death**. <note>Ecclesiastes 7:26; the source cites verse 27</note>

->IX.<-

25. Only fear the Lord, and serve him faithfully with your whole **heart**, for you have seen what great things he does with you. <note>1 Samuel 12:24</note>
26. Hannah prayed: My **heart** rejoices in the Lord, my **horn** is exalted, my **mouth** is opened wide over my enemies, for I rejoice in your **salvation**. <note>1 Samuel 2:1</note>
27. David spoke to Abner: See, here is the **king's** **spear** and the **water jug** that were at his **head**. <note>1 Samuel 26:16</note>

->X.<-

28. Thus **David** became king over all Israel, and he administered justice and **righteousness** to all the people. <note>2 Samuel 8:15</note>
29. The Lord would set a **man** over the congregation of the Lord, so that they may not be like **sheep** without a **shepherd**. <note>Numbers 27:16–17</note>
30. A **man** is tested by the **mouth** of him who praises him, like silver in the **crucible** and gold in the **furnace**. <note>Proverbs 27:21</note>

->XI.<-

31. May the Lord our God not leave us, nor withdraw his **hand** from us, to incline our **heart** to him, that we may walk in all his ways. <note>1 Kings 8:57–58</note>
32. And **David** together with all Israel brought up the **Ark** of the Lord with shouting and **trumpets**. <note>2 Samuel 6:15; source cites 1 Samuel</note>
33. **Wisdom** will beautifully adorn your **head**, and will grace you with a lovely **crown**. <note>Proverbs 4:9</note>

->XII.<-

34. The Lord opened the **eyes** of the **young man**, so that he saw; and behold, the mountain was full of horses and **chariots** of fire all around Elisha. <note>2 Kings 6:17</note>
