35. The godless man does not endure in his **misfortune**, but the righteous is confident even in his **death**. Proverbs of Solomon 14:32. <note>The source cites 16:32, but this text matches the modern Proverbs 14:32.</note>
36. The godless draw the **sword** and bend their **bow** to cast down the miserable and poor, but their **sword** shall enter their own **heart**, and their **bow** shall be broken. Psalm 37:14, 15.

->XIII.<-

37. All Israel brought up the **ark** of the covenant of the Lord with shouting, **horns**, **trumpets**, and loud **cymbals**, with **psalteries** and **harps**. 1 Chronicles 15:28. <note>original: "Posaunen" (horns/trombones) and "Psaltern" (stringed instruments similar to harps). Source cites 17:28.</note>
38. If you seek me with all your **heart**, I will let myself be found by you, says the Lord. Jeremiah 29:13.
39. The Lord searches all **hearts** and understands all the thoughts of the mind; if you seek him, you will find him, but if you forsake him, he will forsake you eternally. 1 Chronicles 28:9. <note>Source cites 29:9.</note>

->XIV.<-

40. The Lord’s **eyes** look upon all lands, that he may strengthen those who are with him with their whole **heart**. 2 Chronicles 16:9.
41. Then Jael took a **nail** from the **tent** and a **hammer** in her **hand**, and went softly to Sisera, and drove the nail into his **temple** so that it sank into the earth. Book of Judges 4:21. <note>original: "Schlaff," meaning the temple or side of the head.</note>
42. God does everything beautifully in his time, and lets the human **heart** be anxious about how things shall go in the **world**. Ecclesiastes 3:11. <note>original: "Prediger Salomo" (The Preacher Solomon).</note>

->XV.<-

43. Ezra prepared his **heart** to seek the **law** of the Lord and to do it and to teach the commandments and rights in Israel. Ezra 7:10.
44. Judas and his men attacked the enemies; with their **hands** they struck, but with their **hearts** they cried out to God. 2 Maccabees 15:26, 27. <note>This is from the Apocrypha, historical books often included in older German Bibles.</note>
45. A good **eye** will be blessed, for he gives of his **bread** to the poor. Proverbs of Solomon 22:9.

->XVI.<-

46. Oh Lord, let your **ears** be attentive and your **eyes** be open, that you may hear the **prayer** of your servant. Nehemiah 1:6.
47. **Hope** that is deferred makes the **heart** sick, but when it 

<column-break/>

comes, that which one desires, it is a **tree** of life. Proverbs 13:12.
48. Incline your **ear** to my speech, let them not depart from your **eyes**, and keep them in your **heart**. Proverbs of Solomon 4:20, 21.

->XVII.<-

49. Haman took the **robe** and the **steed**, and dressed Mordecai, and led him through the city streets, and cried out before him: Thus shall it be done for the **man** whom the king delights to honor. Esther 6:11.
50. The king said: Let Haman hang on the **gallows** fifty cubits high, which he had made for Mordecai. Esther 7:9, 10.
51. Whoever meddles in a stranger's quarrel is like one who pinches a **dog** by the **ears**. Proverbs 26:17.

->XVIII.<-

52. Why is **light** given to the weary, and **life** to the troubled **hearts**, who wait for death and it does not come, who rejoice greatly that they may find the **grave**. Job 3:20, 21.
53. If my **heart** has been secretly enticed, so that my **hand** kissed my **mouth**, I would have denied God from above. Job 31:27, 28. <note>The gesture of "kissing the hand" was a historical form of pagan worship/idolatry.</note>
54. **Man** has his appointed time; the number of his **months** is with God. Job 14:5.

->XIX.<-

55. Praise the Lord, you heavens, and the **waters** that are above the heavens: **fire** and stormy **wind**. Praise him, all people on earth. Psalm 148.
56. My **heart** composes a fine song. I will sing of a **king**; my **tongue** is the pen of a ready **scribe**. Psalm 45:2.
57. It is the Lord who gladdens the **heart** and makes the **face** joyful, and gives health, blessing, and **life**. Sirach 34:20. <note>The source cites chapter 24, but the text corresponds to chapter 34 in most editions of the Wisdom of Sirach.</note>

->XX.<-

58. Wisdom says: All they that hate me love **death**. Proverbs of Solomon 8:36.
59. Pharaoh took his **ring** from his **hand**, and put it on Joseph's **hand**. <note>Genesis 41:42</note>

### XXIX.

85. The Day of the LORD is near in the valley of judgment; the sun and moon shall grow dark, and the stars shall lose their radiance. Joel 3:14–15.
86. For the Lord himself will descend with a battle cry, with the voice of the archangel, and with the trumpet of God; and the dead in Christ will rise first. 1 Thessalonians 4:16.
87. Thus says the Lord GOD: Behold, I will open your graves. Ezekiel 37:12.

### XXX.

88. Hear this, you who say: we want to increase the shekel <note>original: "Seckel," referring to a purse or a specific weight of money.</note> and falsify the scales, to bring the poor under our power for money and the needy for a pair of shoes. Amos 8:5–6.
89. The LORD says: Should I approve of the dishonest scales and the false weights in the purse? Micah 6:11.
90. We growl like bears and moan like doves; for we wait for justice, but it is not there. Isaiah 59:11.

### XXXI.

91. Though you soar aloft like an eagle, and make your nest among the stars, even from there I will cast you down, says the LORD. Obadiah 4.
92. JESUS spoke: I am the light of the world; whoever follows me will not walk in darkness. John 8:12.
93. The hope of the scorner in the time of trouble is like a decayed tooth and a sliding foot. Proverbs of Solomon 25:19.

### XXXII.

94. Jonah prayed: LORD, all your waves and billows passed over me, so that I thought I was cast out from your sight and would no longer see your holy temple. Jonah 2:4–5.

<column-break/>

95. In Damascus, Paul was let down through the wall in a basket out of a window and escaped the hands of the governor. 2 Corinthians 11:32–33.
96. Amend yourself, Jerusalem, lest my heart turn from you and I make you a desolate land where no one dwells. Jeremiah 6:8.

### XXXIII.

97. Alas, it is with me as with one who gleans in the vineyard <note>Gleaning refers to the practice of gathering the leftover grapes after the main harvest is finished.</note> where no grapes are found to eat, and yet would gladly have the best fruit. Micah 7:1.
98. The kingdom of God is like a mustard seed, which a man took and threw into his field; and it grew and became a large tree, and the birds of the heaven dwelt among its branches. Luke 13:19.
99. I will give you a new heart and a new spirit, and I will take the stony heart out of your flesh and give you a heart of flesh, says the LORD. Ezekiel 36:26.

### XXXIV.

100. Woe to the murderous city, where one will hear the cracking of whips and the rattling of wheels, the horses crying out and the chariots rolling. Nahum 3:1–2.
101. Now that we have been made righteous through faith, we have peace with God through our Lord JESUS Christ. Romans 5:1.
102. Let not him who puts on his armor boast like him who has taken it off. 1 Kings 20:11. <note>original: "1. B. Kön." refers to the First Book of Kings.</note>

### XXXV.

103. The earth shall be full of the knowledge of the glory of the LORD, as the waters cover the sea. Habakkuk 2:14. <note>The OCR lists Habakkuk 3:14, but this verse is traditionally found in chapter 2.</note>
104. What no eye has seen, and no ear has heard, and has not entered into the heart of any man, what God has prepared for those who love him. 1 Corinthians 2:9.
105. But the LORD is in his holy temple; let all the world be silent before him. Habakkuk 2:20. <note>The OCR lists Habakkuk 3:20, but this is chapter 2 in standard bibles.</note>

### XXXVI.

106. Seek the **LORD**, all you humble of the land who keep his ordinances; seek **righteousness**, seek **humility**. Zephaniah 2:3.

108. The **Lord** will tend his flock like a **shepherd** and will gather the **lambs** in his **arms**. Isaiah 40:11.

### XXXVII.

109. I will overturn both **chariots** and their **riders**, so that both **horse** and **man** shall fall, each by the other’s **sword**. Haggai 2:23.
110. When Paul gathered a bundle of **brushwood** <note>original: "Reisser"</note> and laid it on the **fire**, a **viper** <note>original: "Otter"</note> came out because of the heat and fastened onto Paul’s **hand**; but he shook the creature into the **fire** and suffered no harm. Acts 28:3, 5.
111. Wild **animals**, **scorpions**, **snakes**, and the **sword** are also created for vengeance, to destroy the ungodly. Sirach 40:36. <note>The book of Sirach is also known as Ecclesiasticus.</note>

### XXXVIII.

112. Rejoice, daughter of Jerusalem! Behold, your **King** comes to you, a righteous one and a helper; he is poor and rides upon a **donkey**. Zechariah 9:9.
113. **Foxes** have **holes** and **birds** under the **heaven** have **nests**, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his **head**. Luke 9:58.
114. **Sword**, awake against my **shepherd**, for I will turn my **hand** toward the little ones. Zechariah 13:7.

### XXXIX.

115. Behold, I will send my **messenger** <note>original: "Engel," which can mean either angel or messenger</note> who shall prepare the way before me, and the **Lord** shall suddenly come to his **temple**. Malachi 3:1.
116. I, **Jesus**, have sent my angel to testify these things to you in the churches; I am the **root** of the lineage of **David**, a bright morning **star**. Revelation 22:16.

<column-break/>

117. To you who fear my name, the **sun** of **righteousness** shall arise with healing beneath its **wings**. Malachi 4:2.

### XL.

118. Remember **Moses**, the servant of the **LORD**, who struck down Amalek not with the **sword**, but with holy **prayer**. Judith 4:12. <note>In many traditions, this refers to the battle where Moses held up his hands in prayer.</note>
119. Let us lift up our **hearts** together with our hands to **God** in **heaven**. Lamentations 3:41.
120. Moses threw the **staff** from him and it became a **snake**; then he reached out his **hand** and caught it, and it became a **staff** in his hand again. Exodus 4:3–4.

### XLI.

121. **God** created **man** for eternal life, but through the envy of the devil, **death** has come into the **world**. Wisdom of Solomon 2:23–24.
122. The **serpent** spoke to the **woman**: "You shall surely not die." And she took of the **fruit** and ate, and gave some to her **husband** also, and he ate. Genesis 3:4, 6.
123. But **Lord**, your **dead** shall live and arise with their **bodies** <note>original: "Leichnam," usually meaning corpse or physical body</note>. Isaiah 26:19.

### XLII.

124. Tobias set out, and a **little dog** ran with him; and he went to wash his **feet** at the river Tigris, and behold, a great **fish** leaped out to swallow him. Tobit 6:1–2.
125. You shall rise up before a gray **head** and honor the **elderly**. Leviticus 19:32.
126. Like a **bird** that sits upon **eggs** but does not hatch them, so is he who gathers wealth unjustly. Jeremiah 17:11.

### XLIII.

127. Simon, the son of Onias, was like the **lilies** by the water, like a beautiful **rose** in **springtime** <note>original: "Lenzen," an archaic term for the spring season</note>. Sirach 50:8.

### XLV.

134. Antiochus entered boldly into the sanctuary and took away the golden **altar**, the **lampstand**, and all that belonged to it, the **table** on which the **showbread** <note>The twelve loaves of "Presence Bread" kept in the Temple</note> lay, the **cups**, **bowls**, and all the **ornament** of the temple, and smashed it all. 1 Maccabees 1:23.
135. The enemy's **arrows** are sharp, and all their **bows** are bent; the hooves of their **steeds** are like **flint** <note>original: "Felsen," rock or flint</note>, and their chariot **wheels** like a **whirlwind**. Isaiah 5:28.
136. The people of the **world** said: Come, let us build ourselves a **city** and a **tower** whose top reaches unto **heaven**. Genesis 11:4.

### XLVI.

137. A terrifying **rider** <note>original: "Reuter"</note> rushed with all his might toward Heliodorus and struck him with his two front **feet**, and two young **men** <note>original: "Gesellen," companions or assistants</note> struck at him courageously. 2 Maccabees 3:25.
138. All **hands** shall grow weary, and every man's **heart** shall be faint; for the **stars** in heaven do not shine brightly, the **sun** rises dark, and the **moon** shines dimly. Isaiah 13:7, 10.

<column-break/>

139. Nicanor’s **head** was displayed, along with the blasphemer's **hand**, which he had stretched out against the holy **house** of the Almighty. 2 Maccabees 15:32.

### XLVII.

140. Esther prayed: O Lord, teach me how I should speak before the **lion** <note>A metaphor for the Persian King Ahasuerus/Xerxes</note>, and turn his **heart** so that he becomes hateful toward our enemy. Additions to Esther 14:12-13. <note>In the German Bible, this is listed as "Stück in Esther 3."</note>
141. A **king** who sits on the **throne** to judge scatters all evil with his **eyes**. Proverbs 20:8.
142. O thou **sword** of the Lord, when wilt thou cease? Return into thy **scabbard**, and rest, and be still. Jeremiah 47:6. <note>Cited as verse 9 in the source text.</note>

### XLVIII.

143. Susanna wept and raised her **eyes** toward **heaven**, for her **heart** had trust in the Lord. History of Susanna v. 35. <note>Susanna is an apocryphal addition to the Book of Daniel.</note>
144. Through wise **women** the **house** is built, but a **foolish woman** tears it down with her own hands. Proverbs 14:1.
145. A bruised **reed** he, my chosen one, shall not utterly break, and the smoldering **wick** he shall not quench, says the Lord. Isaiah 42:1, 3.

### XLIX.

146. Then the priests of **Bel** said: You yourself, Lord **King**, shall set out the **food** and the **drink**, and shut the **door** behind you, and seal it with your **ring**. Bel and the Dragon v. 10.
147. A wicked **man** walks with a perverse **mouth**, winks with his **eyes**, signals with his **feet**, points with his **fingers**, and devises evil in his **heart**. Proverbs 6:12, 13, 14.
148. The dread of a **king** is like the roaring of a young **lion**. Proverbs 20:2.

### L.

149. Daniel answered: But you, Lord **King**, give me permission, and I will slay this **dragon** without any **sword** or **staff**. Bel and the Dragon v. 25.
150. Of what use is **money** in the **hand** of a **fool** to buy **wisdom**, when he is but a **fool**? Proverbs 17:16.

# Third Index.

->Containing the explanation of the emblems presented within the hearts of this book as they appear according to their numbers.<- <note>"Sinn-Bildern" (emblems) refers to the popular 17th-century tradition of using symbolic illustrations to convey moral or religious truths.</note>

5. A heart sparkling like a clear diamond set in gold.
7. Like brotherly pledges of the hand, a peaceful heart.
8. A heart inflamed with the fire of the good Spirit.
13. A heart bound as firmly as if by a true oath.
24. A heart pierced by the arrows of lustful love.
25. A heart signed over to the Highest, as if by letter and seal.
26. A heart praising the Lord with the Davidic harp of praise. <note>A reference to King David, the biblical psalmist.</note>
31. A heart directed toward God by the beloved Cross.
36. A bloodthirsty heart equipped with spear and projectile.
37. A heart carrying the beautiful service of God within itself.
38. Hearts filled with anger, greed, drunkenness, gambling, murder, and vice.
40. A heart offering spiritual incense to the honor of God.
42. A heart wondering and lamenting over the strange course of the world.
43. A heart striving for angelic purity.
44. Hearts persisting with sighs and humble prayer.
47. A heart distressed and fettered as if with a chain.

<column-break/>

48. A heart devoted to the commandments of God.
52. Hearts wounded because of great misery and hardship.
53. A heart obeying God more than men.
56. A heart praising God like a sweet-singing nightingale.
57. A heart gladdened by God, as if refreshed with lovely roses.
65. A heart provided as if with the eyes of watchfulness.
69. A heart encouraged as if with violins and string-music.
72. A heart clamped shut as if with an iron lock.
73. A heart floating like a ship in the stormy waves of the sea.
74. A heart exhausted like withered flowers.
84. Hearts enlightened by the clear mirror of wisdom.
96. A heart beaming with Divine Grace to lure mankind toward improvement.
99. Hearts shining sun-bright with good purpose, avoiding godless stone-like stubbornness, and willing toward the good.

104. The heart desiring the most noble jewel. <note>In emblem literature, the "noble jewel" often represents wisdom, faith, or Christ himself.</note>

213. A heart poisoned as if by unclean spiders.
219. The heart locked shut by righteous Divine wrath.

227. Hearts gleaming from the bright morning star of God’s Word.

## Attached ACROSTIC.
<note>An acrostic is a poem where the first letter of each line spells out a word or name. In this original German poem, the bolded letters spell out the author’s name: **MELCHIOR MATTSPERGER**.</note>

**M**an often imagines many things to himself, because thoughts
**E**ven are permitted and duty-free; yet commonly
**L**something runs alongside that does not stay within the bounds
**C**hristian-minded duty, therefore let everyone
**H**eed [watch] himself the very best, so that his imagination stands
**I**n a firmly-fixed goal, which may be edifying,
**O**utwardly [without] all vanity, upon things that are seen,
**R**eally profitable to be, toward many kinds of virtue.
**M**ay then this little work represent <note>original: "einbilden." This is a play on words; the author is both "imagining" a moral truth and "depicting" it in the book's images.</note> something useful:
**A**in the manner it can, then I am well satisfied,
**T**if it encounters such hearts that allow themselves to be portrayed,
**T**rue, faithful, good works, godly arranged,
**S**o may heaven itself shine upon their minds,
**P**for the Spirit of the Highest is accustomed to instill the good,
**E**it cannot even once vanish from him in its usefulness,
**R**ules and guides those well, who are devoted to Him.
**G**it is therefore already fitting to present this book here,
**E**it teaches, edifies, delights, offers good insights,
**R**ises from God’s Word, may He give His blessing
in Augsburg, and wherever it may further serve.

<vocab>Acrostic, Melchior Mattsperger, Heart, Virtue, Augsburg, Imagination</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes an index of symbolic "heart" emblems, categorizing various spiritual and moral states ranging from the "beast-like" heart to the "enlightened" heart. It concludes with an acrostic poem by the author, Melchior Mattsperger, dedicating the work to the spiritual edification of the reader.</summary>
<keywords>heart emblems, Melchior Mattsperger, acrostic, spiritual discipline, repentance, Augsburg, Christian virtue, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the concluding epilogue for the manuscript. It follows a series of symbolic or "hieroglyphic" explanations, providing a personal reflection by the author on the purpose of their work and a defense against potential critics.</meta>

### Key
->concerning the Hieroglyphic meanings<-

# Concluding Poem

In these times, *writing books* is in *vogue* throughout the world,
So that *one* can drive away enough *gnats* and *idle whims* <note>The original German uses "Muggen" (gnats) and "Grillen" (crickets), which were common metaphors in the 17th and 18th centuries for melancholy, strange fancies, or pestering thoughts.</note>,
And increase *knowledge*: blessed is he who hits the *mark*,
When *hand* and *quill* achieve what adorns the *heart* and *soul*.
Now this *book* before your eyes is not without *flaws*:
Yet it will hopefully be permitted to serve as a *pastime* <term>Kurtzweil; in this period, "pastime" often referred to leisure activities that were both entertaining and morally or intellectually edifying.</term>.
It is nothing *extraordinary*, yet *ancient* in its foundation;
It also *sounds* humble: yet it comes from *God’s mouth*.
Therefore I *heed* not *envy*, the *teeth of critics*, or *barking* <note>original: "Klaffen"; referring to the noisy, empty chatter of detractors.</note>,
Companions whose weapons are mockery and hypocrisy.
In confidence, this was composed for the honor of God alone;
I would *hate* it and be sorry if it should not bear fruit.

<image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament consisting of three floral/scrollwork elements arranged horizontally, with the central piece being larger and more elaborate than the two flanking it.</image-desc>

<summary>This concluding poem reflects on the common practice of book-writing, acknowledging the work's potential flaws while asserting its divine inspiration and purpose as a tool for spiritual edification.</summary>
<keywords>epilogue, poetry, hieroglyphics, defense of work, divine inspiration, edification, authorship</keywords>

# Concerning the Human Spirit and the Soul

## Part One: The Nature of Consciousness

It is an old and established truth that man is a *rational animal* <note>original Latin: "animal rationale"</note>.

->Aristotle teaches us this in his writings.<-

<margin>See the *Metaphysics*</margin>

But what does that actually mean? <term>Reason</term> <note>The original German "Vernunft" refers to the specific human capacity to perceive universal truths and logical connections, distinguishing it from "Verstand" (mere intellect).</note> is not merely the application of logic. **First**, one must consider the *soul* <note>original Greek: "psyche"</note> as the animating principle of the body.

<column-break/>

| Concept | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Spirit | The divine spark within |
| Soul | The seat of emotions and will |

One often finds <insert>in the ancient texts</insert> references to the <unclear>substance</unclear> of the soul. <gloss>The author is likely referring to the Neoplatonic view here.</gloss>

<meta>This page concludes the manuscript with a "Hieroglyphic" poem, a popular Baroque literary device known as a rebus, where images substitute for words or syllables to create a puzzle for the reader.</meta>

# Hieroglyphic Concluding Poem

<image-desc>A rebus poem (hieroglyphic poem) where various words are replaced by small hand-colored illustrations. The text is written in a cursive hand with the drawings integrated into the sentences.</image-desc>

The **[man with a walking stick]** is in the **[world]** in the **[swan]**-g <note>original: "im Schwang," a German idiom meaning "in full swing" or "currently fashionable"</note> of writing **[books]**,
By which **[man]** has **[stars]** <note>light or clarity</note> enough, and can drive **[spider]** <note>melancholy or envy</note> away,
And that he **[scales]** <note>weighs or judges</note> more: Whoever hits the **[target]** is be-**[P with a crown]**-ed <note>original: "bekrönt," meaning crowned or honored</note>,
When **[heart with a knife]** <note>sorrow or passion</note> and **[quill]** reach what **[heart]** and **[letter T]** <note>the word "Herz" (heart) spelled phonetically</note> adorns.
Now, without M-**[man in red]** <note>the word "Mann" (man)</note> this **[open book]** is not here before **[eyes]**,
Yet **[heart]** it heart-**[tower/castle]**-ily <note>original: "herz-schloss-lich," a pun using "Schloss" (castle) for "heartily"</note>, it **[leaf]**-ts <note>original: "erlaubt," meaning permits</note> to serve as a **[pastime/owl]** <note>original: "Kurzweile," a pun using "Eule" (owl) for the second half of the word for "amusement"</note>.
It is nothing **[scissors]** <note>original: "schwere," a pun on "Schere" (scissors) meaning "heavy" or "difficult"</note> the **[coffin]** <note>death</note> yet **[tower/castle]** old in the ground.
It **[spoon]**-s <note>likely "lauffelt" or "runs"</note> also **[small/ring]** <note>original: "gering," meaning slight</note> yet out of **[monk]** <note>original: "manch," meaning many</note> it **[lamp]** <note>shines</note>.
Therefore I do not see **[death]** **[D]**-**[raven]**-**[S]**-**[A]** and **[Kl]**-**[man in a boat]**.
**[B]**-**[donkey]**-en <note>likely "beseelen," meaning to animate or inspire</note> their mockery and **[hat]** <note>protect/guard</note> the **[rose]**.
**[Bench]** <note>likely "Geschenk," meaning gift</note> is this **[composed/barrel]** <note>original: "verfasst," a pun on "Fass" (barrel) meaning "composed"</note> to God's **[letter W]** <note>honor or worth</note> alone.
I **[lamb/leave]** <note>original: "lass," a pun on "Lamm" (lamb)</note> it always, and who to me **[L]**-**[basket]**-s <note>original: "Laster," meaning vices or burdens</note> shall not **[flowers]**-ing <note>original: "hold," meaning friendly or favorable</note> be.

<summary>This page features a Baroque rebus poem that serves as a concluding reflection on the act of writing and the author's devotion to God despite potential critics.</summary>
